========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main reason is that I just can’t stop worrying. It’s like everything in my life is stacked up in my head all at once—my marriage, my job, money, the house, the kids—and I can’t seem to put any of it down.

I work with computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot of details, but lately it feels like I’m constantly thinking I’m slipping at work, like I’m missing things or not keeping up the way I should. Then I come home and I’m thinking about bills, projects around the house that I started and didn’t finish, and whether I’m letting my wife down. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I start wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and a father.

I know everyone worries about stuff, but this feels like it’s all the time, and it’s about everything, not just one problem. I came in because I don’t really know how to shut it off anymore.

Yeah, I mean… not so much stomach problems that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. The biggest thing is I feel wound tight almost all day. It’s like my body is carrying the worry even when I’m trying not to think about it.

I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.

Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.

Like, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.

It happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.

I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more that I can’t relax, and then I get frustrated with myself because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.

I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.

For me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.

So I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.

Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.

I’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.

It’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is that I just can’t stop worrying. It’s like everything in my life is stacked up in my head all at once—my marriage, my job, money, the house, the kids—and I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot of details, but lately it feels like I’m constantly thinking I’m slipping at work, like I’m missing things or not keeping up the way I should. Then I come home and I’m thinking about bills, projects around the house that I started and didn’t finish, and whether I’m letting my wife down. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I start wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and a father.\n\nI know everyone worries about stuff, but this feels like it’s all the time, and it’s about everything, not just one problem. I came in because I don’t really know how to shut it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach problems along with your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much stomach problems that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. The biggest thing is I feel wound tight almost all day. Like my shoulders are locked up, and I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I stop for a second and notice I’m basically bracing myself, like something bad is about to happen.\n\nAnd it’s hard to sit still. At work, between meetings, I’ll catch myself pacing instead of just waiting like a normal person. Or if I’m at chess club, I’ll be tapping the pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there feels almost impossible sometimes. It’s like my body is carrying the worry even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad or down most of the time, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely worn out and kind of discouraged a lot, but it feels more like I’m tense and preoccupied than… depressed, I guess? \n\nLike, I’ll have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just handle normal life like everyone else?” and that can make me feel pretty low. But if you’re asking whether I wake up every day feeling sad all day, I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I can’t relax, and then I get frustrated with myself because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.

My shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.

And sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is that I just can’t stop worrying. It’s like everything in my life is stacked up in my head all at once—my marriage, my job, money, the house, the kids—and I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot of details, but lately it feels like I’m constantly thinking I’m slipping at work, like I’m missing things or not keeping up the way I should. Then I come home and I’m thinking about bills, projects around the house that I started and didn’t finish, and whether I’m letting my wife down. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I start wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and a father.\n\nI know everyone worries about stuff, but this feels like it’s all the time, and it’s about everything, not just one problem. I came in because I don’t really know how to shut it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach problems along with your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much stomach problems that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. The biggest thing is I feel wound tight almost all day. Like my shoulders are locked up, and I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I stop for a second and notice I’m basically bracing myself, like something bad is about to happen.\n\nAnd it’s hard to sit still. At work, between meetings, I’ll catch myself pacing instead of just waiting like a normal person. Or if I’m at chess club, I’ll be tapping the pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there feels almost impossible sometimes. It’s like my body is carrying the worry even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad or down most of the time, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely worn out and kind of discouraged a lot, but it feels more like I’m tense and preoccupied than… depressed, I guess? \n\nLike, I’ll have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just handle normal life like everyone else?” and that can make me feel pretty low. But if you’re asking whether I wake up every day feeling sad all day, I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I can’t relax, and then I get frustrated with myself because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nMy shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s not totally unpredictable, but it’s also not like there’s one clear trigger I can point to and say, “That’s the thing.” It’s more that almost anything can set it off because there are so many things already sitting in my head.

I mean, I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, all these projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. So sometimes there’s an obvious starting point, like an email from work or a bill I forgot about, but other times I’ll just be doing something normal and realize I’m already halfway into this spiral about all of it.

So I guess I usually have some idea what it connects to, but it’s not neat or predictable. It’s like my mind can grab onto almost anything and turn it into proof that I’m not keeping up.

Yes. It’s not just one big dramatic thing, it’s all the normal stuff too, and somehow it all feels urgent at the same time.

Like, I’ll worry about my marriage, and then almost immediately it turns into whether I’m doing enough at home, whether I’m being a good husband, whether I’m failing as a father. Then work comes in, like I’m worried I’m slipping or that people are noticing I’m not keeping up. And then it’s bills, and then the house, and these projects I keep starting and dropping, and I’ll think, “Why can’t I just finish basic things like a normal person?”

So yes, it’s a wide range. It’s everyday things, but they don’t feel everyday in my head. They pile up and connect to this bigger feeling that I’m not managing my life and I’m letting everyone down.

I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nMy shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually know what triggers your episodes of anxiety or are they unpredictable?\n\nPatient: It’s not totally unpredictable, but it’s also not like there’s one clear trigger I can point to and say, “That’s the thing.” It’s more that almost anything can set it off because there are so many things already sitting in my head.\n\nI mean, I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, all these projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. So sometimes there’s an obvious starting point, like an email from work or a bill I forgot about, but other times I’ll just be doing something normal and realize I’m already halfway into this spiral about all of it.\n\nSo I guess I usually have some idea what it connects to, but it’s not neat or predictable. It’s like my mind can grab onto almost anything and turn it into proof that I’m not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about a wide range of things, including everyday, routine matters?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big dramatic thing, it’s all the normal stuff too, and somehow it all feels urgent at the same time.\n\nLike, I’ll worry about my marriage, and then almost immediately it turns into whether I’m doing enough at home, whether I’m being a good husband, whether I’m failing as a father. Then work comes in, like I’m worried I’m slipping or that people are noticing I’m not keeping up. And then it’s bills, and then the house, and these projects I keep starting and dropping, and I’ll think, “Why can’t I just finish basic things like a normal person?”\n\nSo yes, it’s a wide range. It’s everyday things, but they don’t feel everyday in my head. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely more scattered across a lot of different parts of my life. I wish it were just one area, honestly, because then maybe I could deal with that one thing and feel like I had a handle on it.

But it’s like… I came in because I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage is a big one, and then that turns into whether I’m being a good husband, and then whether I’m basically failing as a father. Then work gets mixed in — like, am I slipping, are people noticing, am I falling behind — and then bills, the house, all these projects around the house that I start and then drop or avoid.

It doesn’t stay in one lane. One worry kind of branches into another, and before I know it I’m thinking about five or six things and they all feel connected, like they’re all evidence that I’m not keeping up with my life. So, no, it’s not just a few specific areas. It feels like it spreads across almost everything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main reason is that I just can’t stop worrying. It’s like everything in my life is stacked up in my head all at once—my marriage, my job, money, the house, the kids—and I can’t seem to put any of it down.\n\nI work with computers, so I’m used to juggling a lot of details, but lately it feels like I’m constantly thinking I’m slipping at work, like I’m missing things or not keeping up the way I should. Then I come home and I’m thinking about bills, projects around the house that I started and didn’t finish, and whether I’m letting my wife down. And then it turns into this bigger thing where I start wondering if I’m basically failing as a husband and a father.\n\nI know everyone worries about stuff, but this feels like it’s all the time, and it’s about everything, not just one problem. I came in because I don’t really know how to shut it off anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or stomach problems along with your worries?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… not so much stomach problems that I’ve really tracked, but physically I do feel it. The biggest thing is I feel wound tight almost all day. Like my shoulders are locked up, and I don’t even realize I’m doing it until I stop for a second and notice I’m basically bracing myself, like something bad is about to happen.\n\nAnd it’s hard to sit still. At work, between meetings, I’ll catch myself pacing instead of just waiting like a normal person. Or if I’m at chess club, I’ll be tapping the pieces or moving my hands around because just sitting there feels almost impossible sometimes. It’s like my body is carrying the worry even when I’m trying not to think about it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say sad or down most of the time, exactly. I mean, I’m definitely worn out and kind of discouraged a lot, but it feels more like I’m tense and preoccupied than… depressed, I guess? \n\nLike, I’ll have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just handle normal life like everyone else?” and that can make me feel pretty low. But if you’re asking whether I wake up every day feeling sad all day, I’m not sure that’s the main thing. It’s more that I can’t relax, and then I get frustrated with myself because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nMy shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually know what triggers your episodes of anxiety or are they unpredictable?\n\nPatient: It’s not totally unpredictable, but it’s also not like there’s one clear trigger I can point to and say, “That’s the thing.” It’s more that almost anything can set it off because there are so many things already sitting in my head.\n\nI mean, I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, I do hate when it gets intense, obviously, but my worrying isn’t mainly about having another episode or panic or anything like that.

It’s more like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this constant thought underneath it all that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. That’s what I’m stuck on most of the time.

So it’s not, “What if I have another big fear episode?” It’s more, “What if I’m not keeping up with my life, what if I’m letting everyone down, what if all these things are proof that I’m failing?” It just keeps spreading from one thing to the next.

Then I come home and I’m thinking about bills, projects around the house that I started and didn’t finish, and whether I’m letting my wife down. I mean, I’m definitely worn out and kind of discouraged a lot, but it feels more like I’m tense and preoccupied than… depressed, I guess? \n\nLike, I’ll have moments where I think, “Why can’t I just handle normal life like everyone else?” and that can make me feel pretty low. It’s more that I can’t relax, and then I get frustrated with myself because of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t think I’ve been avoiding things in a really obvious way, like refusing to leave the house or canceling everything. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. It’s more… I’ll hesitate, or I’ll put something off because I don’t want to deal with how worked up I’m going to feel about it.\n\nBut then I also wonder if I’m just making excuses, because some of it looks normal from the outside. Like if there’s something I know is going to make me think about money or work or responsibilities, I might delay dealing with it longer than I should. I don’t know if that counts as avoidance or just procrastination. I still do most things, I think. I’m not really sure how to separate “avoiding” from just feeling overwhelmed and dragging my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the worst parts, I think. It’s not like one single thought repeats word-for-word, exactly, but it’s like one worry gets stuck and then it keeps branching. I’ll be trying to do something normal, like focus on the code in front of me at work, and I’ll tell myself, “Just handle this one task, just look at this one problem,” but then some concern hooks onto another one.\n\nLike, I’ll make a small mistake or I’ll worry that I’m not working fast enough, and suddenly I’m not just thinking about that line of code anymore. I’m thinking, “What if this affects my review?” and then, “What if my manager thinks I’m slipping?” and then it jumps to me losing my job, and then my family being worse off because of me. And by that point I’m twenty steps ahead in my head, imagining this whole disaster, even though nothing has actually happened yet.\n\nIt happens outside work too. I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nMy shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually know what triggers your episodes of anxiety or are they unpredictable?\n\nPatient: It’s not totally unpredictable, but it’s also not like there’s one clear trigger I can point to and say, “That’s the thing.” It’s more that almost anything can set it off because there are so many things already sitting in my head.\n\nI mean, I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, all these projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. So sometimes there’s an obvious starting point, like an email from work or a bill I forgot about, but other times I’ll just be doing something normal and realize I’m already halfway into this spiral about all of it.\n\nSo I guess I usually have some idea what it connects to, but it’s not neat or predictable. It’s like my mind can grab onto almost anything and turn it into proof that I’m not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about a wide range of things, including everyday, routine matters?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big dramatic thing, it’s all the normal stuff too, and somehow it all feels urgent at the same time.\n\nLike, I’ll worry about my marriage, and then almost immediately it turns into whether I’m doing enough at home, whether I’m being a good husband, whether I’m failing as a father. Then work comes in, like I’m worried I’m slipping or that people are noticing I’m not keeping up. And then it’s bills, and then the house, and these projects I keep starting and dropping, and I’ll think, “Why can’t I just finish basic things like a normal person?”\n\nSo yes, it’s a wide range. It’s everyday things, but they don’t feel everyday in my head. They pile up and connect to this bigger feeling that I’m not managing my life and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are focused on a few specific areas of your life or are they more scattered across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more scattered across a lot of different parts of my life. I wish it were just one area, honestly, because then maybe I could deal with that one thing and feel like I had a handle on it.\n\nBut it’s like… I came in because I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage is a big one, and then that turns into whether I’m being a good husband, and then whether I’m basically failing as a father. Then work gets mixed in — like, am I slipping, are people noticing, am I falling behind — and then bills, the house, all these projects around the house that I start and then drop or avoid.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in one lane. One worry kind of branches into another, and before I know it I’m thinking about five or six things and they all feel connected, like they’re all evidence that I’m not keeping up with my life. So, no, it’s not just a few specific areas. It feels like it spreads across almost everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety and worries are mostly about having another episode of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I do hate when it gets intense, obviously, but my worrying isn’t mainly about having another episode or panic or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this constant thought underneath it all that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. That’s what I’m stuck on most of the time.\n\nSo it’s not, “What if I have another big fear episode?” It’s more, “What if I’m not keeping up with my life, what if I’m letting everyone down, what if all these things are proof that I’m failing?” It just keeps spreading from one thing to the next.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s much more tied to daily responsibilities and tasks. I mean, I can feel uncomfortable around people sometimes, but that’s not really the center of it. The reason I came in is because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once.

It’s my marriage, then whether I’m doing enough at home, then whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father. Then my mind jumps to work — like maybe I’m slipping or people can tell I’m not keeping up — and then it goes to bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and not finishing. It’s all these normal life things, but they feel like this huge pile I’m supposed to manage, and I’m constantly afraid I’m dropping the ball somewhere.

So no, I wouldn’t say it’s mainly social situations. It’s more like the ordinary responsibilities of my life just don’t feel ordinary in my head. They feel like proof that I’m not handling things.

I’ll try to breathe through a workout or tell myself to just be present, but my mind keeps pulling me back into some concern. It feels really hard to shut off once it starts, even when I know I’m spiraling.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden, intense episode of fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I don’t think so in the way people describe it, where it suddenly hits out of nowhere and peaks really fast. I do get really uncomfortable and worked up, but it usually feels like it builds because I’ve been thinking about something or worrying in circles, not like a sudden attack that comes on in a few minutes.\n\nI guess there have been times where I realize I’m extremely tense all at once, but when I look back, it’s usually been ramping up for a while and I just didn’t notice how bad it got until I was already in it. So… if you mean a sudden wave of intense fear that peaks within minutes, I don’t know that I’ve had that, or at least I haven’t identified it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were having a heart attack or thought you were going to die during one of these episodes of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. At least, I don’t remember ever thinking, “I’m having a heart attack,” or “I’m about to die,” in that kind of immediate way.\n\nWhen I get really anxious or worked up, it’s more like my mind is racing through consequences or worst-case scenarios, and I feel trapped in the worry, but I don’t usually interpret it as something physically catastrophic happening right that second. I might feel overwhelmed, definitely, but not like I’m dying or need emergency help. If anything, it’s more mental than that, like I can’t get out of the loop.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of intense fear or discomfort that seemed to come on quickly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. If you mean something that comes on really suddenly, like I’m fine and then within a few minutes I’m intensely afraid, I don’t think that’s really how it usually happens for me.\n\nFor me it’s more like I realize I’ve been getting increasingly uncomfortable, and by the time I notice it, it can feel pretty intense. But when I think back, there’s usually been some buildup. It’s not like a switch flips out of nowhere. It’s more gradual, even if I don’t always catch it early.\n\nSo I guess my answer is… not really, or at least I haven’t noticed it happening in that quick, sudden way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries and anxiety are present most days, regardless of what you're doing or where you are?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually why I came in, because it feels like it’s just… there most days, no matter what I’m doing. It’s not one single thing I worry about and then it’s done. It’s like everything is stacked up at once.\n\nI’ll be at work trying to focus, and I’m worrying that I’m slipping or missing something. Then it shifts to bills, or the house projects I keep starting and dropping, or whether I’m being a bad husband, or whether I’m failing as a father. Even if I’m technically doing something else, like sitting with my family or trying to get through the day, it’s still running in the background.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because it’s not always tied to where I am. I can be at home, at work, driving, whatever, and my mind just keeps circling through all of it. My marriage, work, money, the house, the kids… and it all turns into this bigger fear that I’m not keeping up and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. Honestly, that’s one of the things I notice the most physically. I feel kind of wound tight almost all day, like I’m bracing for something even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nMy shoulders especially are just… locked up a lot of the time. I’ll realize I’m sitting at my desk with them up near my ears, or my jaw tight, and I have to consciously tell myself to loosen up. But then a few minutes later I’m doing it again.\n\nAnd sitting still is hard. Between meetings at work I’ll catch myself pacing around instead of just waiting normally. Or even at chess club, where you’d think I’d be calm and focused, I’m tapping pieces or shifting around because it feels almost impossible to just sit there quietly. It’s not always obvious to other people, maybe, but inside it feels like my body is constantly ready for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually know what triggers your episodes of anxiety or are they unpredictable?\n\nPatient: It’s not totally unpredictable, but it’s also not like there’s one clear trigger I can point to and say, “That’s the thing.” It’s more that almost anything can set it off because there are so many things already sitting in my head.\n\nI mean, I came in because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, all these projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this bigger fear that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. So sometimes there’s an obvious starting point, like an email from work or a bill I forgot about, but other times I’ll just be doing something normal and realize I’m already halfway into this spiral about all of it.\n\nSo I guess I usually have some idea what it connects to, but it’s not neat or predictable. It’s like my mind can grab onto almost anything and turn it into proof that I’m not keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about a wide range of things, including everyday, routine matters?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just one big dramatic thing, it’s all the normal stuff too, and somehow it all feels urgent at the same time.\n\nLike, I’ll worry about my marriage, and then almost immediately it turns into whether I’m doing enough at home, whether I’m being a good husband, whether I’m failing as a father. Then work comes in, like I’m worried I’m slipping or that people are noticing I’m not keeping up. And then it’s bills, and then the house, and these projects I keep starting and dropping, and I’ll think, “Why can’t I just finish basic things like a normal person?”\n\nSo yes, it’s a wide range. It’s everyday things, but they don’t feel everyday in my head. They pile up and connect to this bigger feeling that I’m not managing my life and I’m letting everyone down.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries are focused on a few specific areas of your life or are they more scattered across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more scattered across a lot of different parts of my life. I wish it were just one area, honestly, because then maybe I could deal with that one thing and feel like I had a handle on it.\n\nBut it’s like… I came in because I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage is a big one, and then that turns into whether I’m being a good husband, and then whether I’m basically failing as a father. Then work gets mixed in — like, am I slipping, are people noticing, am I falling behind — and then bills, the house, all these projects around the house that I start and then drop or avoid.\n\nIt doesn’t stay in one lane. One worry kind of branches into another, and before I know it I’m thinking about five or six things and they all feel connected, like they’re all evidence that I’m not keeping up with my life. So, no, it’s not just a few specific areas. It feels like it spreads across almost everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety and worries are mostly about having another episode of intense fear or discomfort?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I do hate when it gets intense, obviously, but my worrying isn’t mainly about having another episode or panic or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and then dropping, and then this constant thought underneath it all that I’m basically failing as a husband and father. That’s what I’m stuck on most of the time.\n\nSo it’s not, “What if I have another big fear episode?” It’s more, “What if I’m not keeping up with my life, what if I’m letting everyone down, what if all these things are proof that I’m failing?” It just keeps spreading from one thing to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in social situations or is it more related to your daily responsibilities and tasks?\n\nPatient: It’s much more tied to daily responsibilities and tasks. I mean, I can feel uncomfortable around people sometimes, but that’s not really the center of it. The reason I came in is because I feel like I can’t stop worrying about everything at once.\n\nIt’s my marriage, then whether I’m doing enough at home, then whether I’m basically failing as a husband and father. Then my mind jumps to work — like maybe I’m slipping or people can tell I’m not keeping up — and then it goes to bills, the house, the projects I keep starting and not finishing. It’s all these normal life things, but they feel like this huge pile I’m supposed to manage, and I’m constantly afraid I’m dropping the ball somewhere.\n\nSo no, I wouldn’t say it’s mainly social situations. It’s more like the ordinary responsibilities of my life just don’t feel ordinary in my head. They feel like proof that I’m not handling things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s exactly what it feels like. It’s not like I’m fixated on some rare disaster or one big unusual thing happening. It’s the everyday stuff — the normal life things that other people seem to just handle — that I can’t stop turning over in my head.

I came in because I can’t stop worrying about everything at once. My marriage, whether I’m slipping at work, bills, the house, all the house projects I keep starting and then dropping or avoiding… and then underneath all of that is this constant feeling that I’m basically failing as a husband and father.

So, yeah, it’s not extraordinary events. It’s more like regular responsibilities, but my mind treats them like they’re all urgent and connected, like if I’m behind on one thing it means I’m falling apart in every area.
